Table 3:
Sparsification Hardness

In this section, we show the following two hardness results:

Theorem 8.2

(Low-dimensional sparsification hardness)
.

Consider a decreasing function
f:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}

that is not
(\rho,L)
-multiplicatively lipschitz for some
L>1
, where
c
and
c_{0}
are the constants given in Theorem
3.25
and
\rho=1+2\log(10L^{1/(4c_{0})})/L
. There is no algorithm that, given a set of
n
points
X
in
d=c^{\log^{*}n}
dimensions, returns a sparsifier of the
f
-graph for
X
in less than
O(nL^{1/(8c_{0})})
time assuming
SETH
.

Theorem 8.3

(High-dimensional sparsification hardness)
.

Consider a decreasing function
f:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}

that is not
(\rho,L)
-multiplicatively Lipschitz for some
L>1
, where
\rho=1+2\log(10(2^{L^{0.48}}))/L
. There is no algorithm that, given a set of
n
points
X
in
d=O(\log n)
dimensions, returns a sparsifier of the
f
-graph for
X
in less than
O(n2^{L^{.48}})
time assuming
SETH
.

Both of these results follow from the following reduction:

Lemma 8.4
.

Consider a decreasing function
f:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
that is not
(\rho,L)
-multiplicatively Lipschitz for some
L>1
, where
\rho=1+2\log(10n)/L
and
n>1
. Suppose that there is an algorithm
\mathcal{A}
that, when given a set of
n
points
X\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
, returns a 2-approximate sparsifier for the
f
-graph of
X
with
O(n)
edges in
\mathcal{T}(n,L,d)
time. Then, there is an algorithm (Algorithm
9
) that, given two sets
A,B\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
for which
A\cup B
is
\rho
-spaced with distance set
S
,
k\in S
, and
|A\cup B|=n
, returns whether or not
\min_{a\in A,b\in B}\|a-b\|_{2}\leq k
in

\displaystyle O(\mathcal{T}(|A\cup B|,L,d)+|A\cup B|)

time.

The reduction described starts by scaling the points in
A\cup B
by a factor of
x_{0}/k
to obtain
\widetilde{A}
and
\widetilde{B}
respectively. Then, it sparsifies the
f
-graph for
\widetilde{A}\cup\widetilde{B}
. Finally, it computes the weight of the edges in the
\widetilde{A}
-
\widetilde{B}
cut. Because
f
is not multiplicatively Lipschitz and the distance set for
\widetilde{A}\cup\widetilde{B}
is spaced, thresholding suffices for solving the
A\times B
nearest neighbor problem.

Proof of Lemma
8.4
.

Consider the following algorithm,
BichromaticNearestNeighbor
(Algorithm
9
), given below:

Algorithm 9

1:

procedure

BichromaticNearestNeighbor
(
A,B,k
)
\triangleright
Lemma
8.4

2:

Given
:
A,B\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
with the property that
A\cup B
is
\rho
-spaced, where
\rho=1+2(\log(10n))/L
, and
k\in S

3:

Returns
: whether there are
a\in A,b\in B
for which
\|a-b\|_{2}\leq k

4:

\widetilde{A}\leftarrow\{a\cdot\sqrt{x_{0}}/k\penalty\ |\penalty\ \forall a\in A\}

\triangleright

\widetilde{A}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d}

5:

\widetilde{B}\leftarrow\{b\cdot\sqrt{x_{0}}/k\penalty\ |\penalty\ \forall b\in B\}

\triangleright

\widetilde{B}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d}

6:

H\leftarrow\mathcal{A}(\widetilde{A}\cup\widetilde{B})

\triangleright

H
is a 2-approximate sparsifier for the
f
-graph
G
of
\widetilde{A}\cup\widetilde{B}

7:

if
the total weight of edges between
\widetilde{A}
and
\widetilde{B}
in
H
is at least
f(x_{0})/2

then

8:

return

\mathsf{true}

9:

else

10:

return

\mathsf{false}

11:

end

if

12:

end

procedure

We start by bounding the runtime of this algorithm. Constructing
\widetilde{A}
and
\widetilde{B}
and calculating the total weight of edges between
\widetilde{A}
and
\widetilde{B}
takes
O(n)
time since
H
has
O(n)
edges. Since the sparsification algorithm is only called once, the total runtime is therefore
\mathcal{T}(n,L,d)+O(n)
, as desired. For the rest of the proof, we may therefore focus on correctness.

First, suppose that
\min_{a\in A,b\in B}\|a-b\|_{2}\leq k
. There exists a pair of points
\widetilde{a}\in\widetilde{A}
,
\widetilde{b}\in\widetilde{B}
with
\|\widetilde{a}-\widetilde{b}\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{x_{0}}
. Since
f
is a decreasing function, the edge between
\widetilde{a}
and
\widetilde{b}
in
G
has weight at least
f(x_{0})
, which means that the total weight of edges in the
\widetilde{A}
-
\widetilde{B}
cut in
G
is at least
f(x_{0})
. Since
H
is a 2-approximate sparsifier for
G
, the total weight of edges in the
\widetilde{A}
-
\widetilde{B}
cut is at least
f(x_{0})/2
. This means that
\mathsf{true}
is returned, as desired.

Next, suppose that
\min_{a\in A,b\in B}\|a-b\|_{2}>k
. Since
A\cup B
is
\rho
-spaced with distance set
S
and
k\in S
,
\|a-b\|_{2}\geq\rho\cdot k
for all
a\in A
and
b\in B
. Therefore,
\|\widetilde{a}-\widetilde{b}\|_{2}\geq\rho\cdot\sqrt{x_{0}}
for all
\widetilde{a}\in\widetilde{A}
and
\widetilde{b}\in\widetilde{B}
.

Since
f
is decreasing and not
(\rho,L)
-multiplicatively Lipschitz, the weight of any edge between
\widetilde{A}
and
\widetilde{B}
in
G
is at most

\displaystyle f(\rho\cdot x_{0})\leq f(x_{0})/(100n^{2}).

The total weight of edges between
\widetilde{A}
and
\widetilde{B}
is therefore at most

\displaystyle n^{2}\cdot(f(x_{0})/(100n^{2}))<f(x_{0})/8.

Since
H
is a 2-approximate sparsifier for
G
, the total weight between
C
and
D
is at most
f(x_{0})/4<f(x_{0})/2
, so the algorithm returns
\mathsf{false}
, as desired.
∎

We now prove the theorems:

Proof of Theorem
8.2
.

Consider an instance of
\ell_{2}
-bichromatic closest pair for
n=L^{1/(4c_{0})}
and
d=c^{\log^{*}n}
, where
c
is the constant given in the dimension bound of Theorem
3.25
and
c_{0}
is such that integers have bit length
c_{0}\log n
in Theorem
3.25
. This consists of two sets of points
A,B\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
|A\cup B|=n
for which we wish to compute
\min_{a\in A,b\in B}\|a-b\|_{2}
. By Theorem
3.25
, the coordinates of points in
A
are also
c_{0}\log n
bit integers. Therefore, the set
S
of possible
\ell_{2}
distances between points in
A
and
B
is a set of square roots of integers with
\log d+c_{0}\log n\leq 2c_{0}\log n
bits. Therefore,
S
is a
\rho
-discrete (recall
\rho=1+(2\log(10n))/L
), since
1+1/n^{2c_{0}}>1+(2\log(10n))/L
. Furthermore, note that
f
is not
(\rho,L)
-multiplicatively Lipschitz.

We now describe an algorithm for solving
\ell_{2}
-closest pair on
A\times B
. Use binary search on the values in
S
to compute the minimum distance between points in
A,B
. For each query point
k\in S
, by Lemma
8.4
, there is a

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(n,L,d)=O(\mathcal{T}(L^{1/(4c_{0})},L,c^{\log^{*}L})+L^{1/(4c_{0})})

-time algorithm for determining whether or not the closest pair has distance at most
k
. Therefore, there is a

\displaystyle O(\log|S|\cdot(\mathcal{T}(L^{1/(4c_{0})},L,c^{\log^{*}L})+L^{1/4c_{0}}))=\widetilde{O}(L^{1/(4c_{0})}L^{1/(8c_{0})})<O(n^{3/2})

time algorithm for solving
\ell_{2}
-closest pair on pairs of sets with
n
points. But this is impossible given
SETH
by Theorem
3.25
, a contradiction. This completes the result.
∎

Proof of Theorem
8.3
.

Consider an instance of bichromatic Hamming nearest neighbor search for
n=2^{L^{0.49}}
and
d=c_{1}\log n
for the constant
c_{1}
in the dimension bound in Theorem
3.21
. This consists of two sets of points
A,B\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
|A\cup B|=n
for which we wish to compute
\min_{a\in A,b\in B}\|a-b\|_{2}
. The coordinates of points in
A
and
B
are 0-1. Therefore, the set
S
of possible
\ell_{2}
distances between points in
A
and
B
is the set of square roots of integers between 0 and
c_{1}\log n
, which differ by a factor of at least
1+1/(2c_{1}\log n)>\rho
(recall
\rho=1+(2\log(10n))/L
). Therefore,
A\cup B
is
\rho
-spaced. Note that
f
is also no
(\rho,L)
-multiplicatively Lipschitz by definition.

We now give an algorithm for solving
\ell_{2}
-closest pair on
A\times B
. Use binary search on
S
. For each query
k\in S
, Lemma
8.4
implies that one can check if there is a pair with distance at most
k
in

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(n,L,d)=

\displaystyle\penalty\ O(\mathcal{T}(2^{L^{.49}},L,c_{1}L^{.49})+2^{L^{.49}})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ O(2^{L^{.49}+L^{.48}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ n^{1+o(1)}

time on pairs of sets with
n
points. But this is impossible given
SETH
by Theorem
3.21
. This completes the result.
∎

Next, we prove hardness results for solving Laplacian systems. In these hardness results, we insist that kernels are bounded:

Definition 8.5
.

Call a function
f:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}

g(p)

for a function
g:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
iff for any pair
a,b>0
with
b>a
,
f(b)\geq f(a)\cdot g(b/a)
. Call a set
S\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}

\gamma
-bounded
iff
\max_{s\in S}s\leq\gamma\min_{s\in S,s\neq 0}s
. A set of points
X\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
is called
\gamma
-boxed
iff the set of distances between points in
X
is
\gamma
-bounded.

We show the following results:

Theorem 8.6

(Partial low-dimensional linear system hardness)
.

Consider a decreasing
g(p)=p
-bounded function
f:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}

that is not
(\rho,L)

L>1
, where
c
and
c_{0}
are the constants given in Theorem
3.25
and
\rho=1+16\log(10(L^{1/(32c_{0})}))/L
. Assuming
SETH
, there is no algorithm that, given a
\gamma
-boxed set of
n

X
in
d=c^{\log^{*}n}
dimensions with
f
-graph
G
and a vector
b\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, returns a
\varepsilon=1/(g(\gamma)^{3}2^{\mathrm{poly}(\log n)})
-approximate solution
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
to the geometric Laplacian system
L_{G}x=b
in less than
O(n\log(g(\gamma))L^{1/(64c_{0})})

Theorem 8.7

(Partial high-dimensional linear system hardness)
.

Consider a decreasing
g(p)=e^{p}
-bounded function
f:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
that is not
(\rho,L)
-multiplicatively Lipschitz for some
L>1
, where
\rho=1+16\log(10(2^{L^{0.48}}))/L
. Assuming
SETH
, there is no algorithm that, given a
\gamma
-boxed set of
n
points
X
in
d=O(\log n)

f
-graph
G
and a vector
b\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, returns a
\varepsilon=1/(g(\gamma)^{3}2^{\mathrm{poly}(\log n)})
-approximate solution
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
to the geometric Laplacian system
L_{G}x=b
in less than
O(n\log(g(\gamma))2^{L^{.48}})
time assuming
SETH
.

To prove these theorems, we use the following reduction from bichromatic nearest neighbors:

Lemma 8.8
.

Consider a decreasing
g(p)
-bounded function
f:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
that is not
(\rho,L)
-multiplicatively Lipschitz for some
L>1
, where
\rho=1+16(\log(10n))/L
and
n>1
. Suppose that there is an algorithm
\mathcal{A}
that, given a
\gamma
-boxed set of
n
points
X
in
d
dimensions with
f
-graph
G
and a vector
b\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, returns an
\varepsilon=1/(g(\gamma)^{3}2^{\mathrm{poly}(\log n)})
-approximate solution
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
to the geometric Laplacian system
L_{G}x=b
in
\mathcal{T}(n,L,g(\gamma),d)
time. Then, there is an algorithm that, given two sets
A,B\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
for which
A\cup B
is
\rho
-spaced with
|S|^{O(1)}
-bounded distance set
S
,
k\in S
, and
|A\cup B|=n
, returns whether or not
\min_{a\in A,b\in B}\|a-b\|_{2}\leq k
in

\displaystyle O((\log n)\mathcal{T}(n,L,g(|S|^{O(1)}),d)+|A\cup B|)

time.

This reduction works in a similar way to the effective resistance data structure of Spielman and Srivastava
[
SS11
]
, but with minor differences due to the fact that their data structure requires multiplication by incidence matrix of the graph, which in our case is dense. Our reduction uses Johnson-Lindenstrauss to embed the points

\displaystyle v_{s}=L^{\dagger}b_{s}

for vertices
s
in the graph
G
into
O(\log n)
dimensions in a way that distorts the distances

\displaystyle b_{st}^{\top}(L^{\dagger})^{2}b_{st}

for vertices
s,t
in
G
by a factor of at most 2. After computing this embedding, we build an
O(\log n)
-approximate nearest neighbor data structure on the resulting points. This allows us to determine whether or not a vertex in
A
has a high-weight edge in
G
to
B
in almost-constant time. After looping through all of the edges in
A
in total time
n^{1+o(1)}
, we determine whether or not there are any high-weight edges between
A
and
B
in
G
, allowing us to answer the bichromatic nearest neighbors decision problem.

We start by proving a result that links norms of
v_{s}
to effective resistances:

Proposition 8.9
.

In an
n
-vertex graph
G
with vertices
s
and
t
,

\displaystyle 0.5\cdot(\mathtt{Reff}_{G}(s,t))^{2}\leq\|v_{s}-v_{t}\|_{2}^{2}\leq n\cdot(\mathtt{Reff}_{G}(s,t))^{2}

Proof.

Lower bound.

Let
x=v_{t}-v_{s}=L_{G}^{\dagger}b_{st}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. By definition,

\displaystyle\|v_{s}-v_{t}\|_{2}^{2}=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \|x\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\penalty\ (x_{s})^{2}+(x_{t})^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ (x_{s})^{2}+(x_{s}-b_{st}^{\top}L_{G}^{\dagger}b_{st})^{2}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\penalty\ (b_{st}^{\top}L_{G}^{\dagger}b_{st})^{2}/2,

where third step follows from
x_{t}=x_{s}-b_{st}^{\top}L_{G}^{\dagger}b_{st}
.

Thus we complete the proof of the lower bound.

Upper bound.

Next, we prove the upper bound. The maximum and minimum coordinates of
x
are
x_{t}
and
x_{s}
respectively. By definition of the pseudoinverse,
\text{image}(L_{G}^{\dagger})=\text{image}(L_{G})
. Therefore,
\textbf{1}^{\top}x=0
,
x_{s}\leq 0
, and
x_{t}\geq 0
.
x_{s}\leq 0
implies that for all
i\in[n]
,

\displaystyle x_{i}\leq x_{s}+b_{st}^{\top}L_{G}^{\dagger}b_{st}\leq b_{st}^{\top}L_{G}^{\dagger}b_{st}.

x_{t}\geq 0
implies that for all
i\in[n]
,

\displaystyle x_{i}\geq x_{t}-b_{st}^{\top}L_{G}^{\dagger}b_{st}\geq-b_{st}^{\top}L_{G}^{\dagger}b_{st}.

Therefore,
|x_{i}|\leq b_{st}^{\top}L_{G}^{\dagger}b_{st}=\mathtt{Reff}_{G}(s,t)
for all
i\in[n]
. Summing across
i\in[n]
yields the desired upper bound.
∎

Furthermore, the minimum effective resistance of an edge across a cut is related to the maximum weight edge across the cut:

Proposition 8.10
.

In an
m
-edge graph
G
with vertex set
S
,

\displaystyle\min_{s\in S,t\notin S}\mathtt{Reff}_{G}(s,t)\leq\min_{e\in\partial S}(1/w_{e})\leq m\min_{s\in S,t\notin S}\mathtt{Reff}_{G}(s,t).

Proof.

Lower bound.

The lower bound on
\min_{e}1/w_{e}
follows immediately from the fact that for any edge
e=\{s,t\}
,
\mathtt{Reff}_{G}(s,t)\leq r_{e}
.

Upper bound.

For the upper bound, recall that

\displaystyle\mathtt{Reff}_{G}(s,t)

\displaystyle=\min_{f\in\mathbb{R}^{m}:B^{\top}f=b_{st}}\sum_{e\in E(G)}f_{e}^{2}/w_{e}

\displaystyle\geq\min_{f\in\mathbb{R}^{m}:B^{\top}f=b_{st}}\sum_{e\in\partial S}f_{e}^{2}/w_{e}

\displaystyle\geq\left(\min_{f\in\mathbb{R}^{m}:B^{\top}f=b_{st}}\sum_{e\in\partial S}f_{e}^{2}\right)\left(\min_{e\in\partial S}1/w_{e}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{|\partial S|}\left(\min_{f\in\mathbb{R}^{m}:B^{\top}f=b_{st}}\sum_{e\in\partial S}|f_{e}|\right)^{2}\left(\min_{e\in\partial S}1/w_{e}\right)

where the first step follows from definition of effective resistance,
the third step follows from taking
w
out, and the last step follows from Cauchy-Schwarz.

Since
s\in S
and
t\notin S
,
\sum_{e\in\partial S}|f_{e}|\geq 1
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathtt{Reff}_{G}(s,t)\geq\frac{1}{m}\min_{e\in\partial S}1/w_{e}

completing the upper bound.
∎

Proposition 8.11
.

Consider an
n
-vertex connected graph
G
with edge weights
\{w_{e}\}_{e\in G}
, two matrices
Z,\widetilde{Z}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times n}
with rows
\{z_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k}
and
\{\widetilde{z}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k}
respectively for
k\leq n
, and
\varepsilon\in(1/n,1)
. Suppose that both of the following properties hold:

1.

\|z_{i}-\widetilde{z}_{i}\|_{L_{G}}\leq 0.01n^{-12}w_{\min}^{2}w_{\max}^{-2}\|z_{i}\|_{L_{G}}

for all
i\in[k]
, where
w_{\min}
and
w_{\max}
are the minimum and maximum weights of edges in
G
respectively

2.

(1-\varepsilon/10)\cdot\|L_{G}^{\dagger}b_{st}\|_{2}\leq\|Zb_{st}\|_{2}\leq(1+\varepsilon/10)\cdot\|L_{G}^{\dagger}b_{st}\|
for any vertices
s,t
in
G

Then for any vertices
s,t
in
G
,

\displaystyle(1-\varepsilon)\cdot\|L_{G}^{\dagger}b_{st}\|_{2}\leq\|\widetilde{Z}b_{st}\|_{2}\leq(1+\varepsilon)\cdot\|L_{G}^{\dagger}b_{st}\|_{2}.

Proof.

We start by bounding

\displaystyle((z_{i}-\widetilde{z}_{i})^{\top}b_{st})^{2}

for each
i\in[k]
. Since
G
is connected, there is a path from
s
to
t
consisting of edges
e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{\ell}
in that order, where
\ell\leq n
. By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,

\displaystyle((z_{i}-\widetilde{z}_{i})^{\top}b_{st})^{2}\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ n\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}((z_{i}-\widetilde{z}_{i})^{\top}b_{e_{j}})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ (n/w_{\min})\cdot\|z_{i}-\widetilde{z}_{i}\|_{L_{G}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ 0.01n^{-23}w_{\min}^{3}w_{\max}^{-4}\cdot\|z_{i}\|_{L_{G}}^{2}

where the last step from property 1 in proposition statement.

By the upper bound on
\|Zb_{s^{\prime}t^{\prime}}\|_{2}
for any vertices
s^{\prime},t^{\prime}
in
G
,
(z_{i}^{\top}b_{e})^{2}\leq(1+\varepsilon)^{2}b_{e}^{\top}(L_{G}^{\dagger})^{2}b_{e}
for all edges
e
in
G
, so

\displaystyle 0.01n^{-23}w_{\min}^{3}w_{\max}^{-4}\cdot\|z_{i}\|_{L_{G}}^{2}=

\displaystyle\penalty\ 0.01n^{-23}w_{\min}^{3}w_{\max}^{-4}\cdot\sum_{e\in E(G)}w_{e}((z_{i})^{\top}b_{e})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ 0.01n^{-23}w_{\min}^{3}w_{\max}^{-3}\cdot\sum_{e\in E(G)}((z_{i})^{\top}b_{e})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ 0.01n^{-23}w_{\min}^{3}w_{\max}^{-3}\cdot\sum_{e\in E(G)}(1+\varepsilon)^{2}b_{e}^{\top}(L_{G}^{\dagger})^{2}b_{e}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ 0.01n^{-21}w_{\min}^{3}w_{\max}^{-3}(1+\varepsilon)^{2}\max_{e\in E(G)}(b_{e}^{\top}(L_{G}^{\dagger})^{2}b_{e})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ 0.04n^{-21}w_{\min}^{3}w_{\max}^{-3}\max_{e\in E(G)}(b_{e}^{\top}(L_{G}^{\dagger})^{2}b_{e}).

where the first step follows from
\|z_{i}\|_{L_{G}}^{2}=\sum_{e\in E}z_{i}^{\top}w_{e}b_{e}b_{e}^{\top}z_{i}
, the second step follows from
w_{\max}=\max_{e\in G}w_{e}
, and the third step follows from
(z_{i}^{\top}b_{e})^{2}\leq(1+\varepsilon)^{2}b_{e}^{\top}(L_{G}^{\dagger})b_{e}
, the forth step follows from summation has at most
n^{2}
terms, the last step follows from
(1+\varepsilon)^{2}\leq 4
,
\forall\varepsilon\in(0,1)
.

L_{G}^{\dagger}b_{e}
is a vector that is maximized and minimized at the endpoints of
e
. Furthermore,
\textbf{1}\in\text{kernel}(L_{G}^{\dagger})
by definition of the pseudoinverse. Thus, we know
L_{G}^{\dagger}b_{e}
has both positive and negative coordinates and that
\|L_{G}^{\dagger}b_{e}\|_{\infty}\leq\max_{i\neq j}|(L_{G}^{\dagger}b_{e})_{i}-(L_{G}^{\dagger}b_{e})_{j}|\leq b_{e}^{\top}L_{G}^{\dagger}b_{e}
.

We have

\displaystyle\penalty\ 0.04n^{-21}w_{\min}^{3}w_{\max}^{-3}\cdot\max_{e\in E(G)}b_{e}^{\top}(L_{G}^{\dagger})^{2}b_{e}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ 0.04n^{-20}w_{\min}^{3}w_{\max}^{-3}\cdot\max_{e\in E(G)}(b_{e}^{\top}L_{G}^{\dagger}b_{e})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ 0.4n^{-20}w_{\min}w_{\max}^{-3}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ 0.4n^{-16}w_{\min}w_{\max}^{-1}\cdot(b_{st}^{\top}L_{G}^{\dagger}b_{st})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ 0.8n^{-16}w_{\min}w_{\max}^{-1}\cdot\|L_{G}^{\dagger}b_{st}\|_{2}^{2}

where the first step follows from
\max_{e\in E(G)}b_{e}^{\top}(L_{G}^{\dagger})^{2}b_{e}=\max_{e\in E}\|L_{G}^{\dagger}b_{e}\|_{2}^{2}\leq n\max_{e\in E}\|L_{G}^{\dagger}b_{e}\|_{\infty}^{2}\leq n\max_{e\in E(G)}(b_{e}^{\top}L_{G}^{\dagger}b_{e})^{2}
, the second step follows from
\max_{e}(b_{e}^{\top}L_{G}^{\dagger}b_{e})^{2}\leq w_{\min}^{-2}
(the lower bound in Proposition
8.10
), and the third step follows from
w_{\max}^{-2}\leq n^{4}(b_{st}L_{G}^{\dagger}b_{st})^{2}
(the upper bound in Proposition
8.10
), and the last step follows from
(b_{st}^{\top}L_{G}^{\dagger}b_{st})^{2}\leq 2\|L_{G}^{\dagger}b_{st}\|_{2}^{2}
(Since
L_{G}^{\dagger}b_{st}
is maximized and minimized at
t
and
s
respectively).

Combining these inequalities shows that

\displaystyle((z_{i}-\widetilde{z}_{i})^{\top}b_{st})^{2}\leq 0.8n^{-16}w_{\min}w_{\max}^{-1}\cdot\|L_{G}^{\dagger}b_{st}\|_{2}^{2}

Summing over all
i
and using the fact that
k\leq n
,
\varepsilon>1/n
, and
w_{\min}\leq w_{\max}
shows that

\displaystyle\|(Z-\widetilde{Z})b_{st}\|_{2}^{2}=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sum_{i=1}^{k}((z_{i}-\widetilde{z}_{i})^{\top}b_{st})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ 0.8kn^{-16}w_{\min}w_{\max}^{-1}\cdot\|L_{G}^{\dagger}b_{st}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ 0.8\varepsilon^{2}n^{-13}w_{\min}w_{\max}^{-1}\cdot\|L_{G}^{\dagger}b_{st}\|_{2}^{2}\penalty\

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ 0.8\varepsilon^{2}n^{-13}\cdot\|L_{G}^{\dagger}b_{st}\|_{2}^{2}

Combining this with the given upper and lower bounds on
\|Zb_{st}\|_{2}
using the triangle inequality yields the desired result.
∎

Proof of Lemma
8.8
.

Consider the following algorithm
BichromaticNearestNeighbor
, given below:

Algorithm 10

procedure

(

)
\triangleright

2:

of

\triangleright

, i.e.

and

for all

procedure

First, we bound the runtime of
BichromaticNearestNeighborProj
(Algorithm
10
). Computing the sets
C,D
and the matrix
P
trivially takes
\widetilde{O}(n)
time. Computing the matrix
\widetilde{Z}
takes

\displaystyle O(\log n)\mathcal{T}(n,L,g(|S|^{O(1)}),d)

time since the point set
C\cup D
is
|S|^{O(1)}
-boxed. Computing
\widehat{C}
and
\widehat{D}
takes
\widetilde{O}(n)
time, as computing
\widehat{Z}b_{i}
takes
O(\log n)
time for each
i\in C\cup D
since
b_{i}
is supported on just one vertex. Computing
t
takes
n^{1+o(1)}
time by Theorem
3.17
. In particular, one computes
t
by preprocessing a
O(\log n)
-approximate nearest neighbors data structure on
\widehat{D}
(takes
n^{1+o(1)}
time), queries the data structure on all points in
\widehat{C}
(takes
n(n^{o(1)})=n^{1+o(1)}
time), and returns the minimum of all of the queries. The subsequent if statement takes constant time. Therefore, the reduction takes

\displaystyle O((\log n)\cdot\mathcal{T}(n,L,|S|^{O(1)},d)+n^{1+o(1)}

time overall, as desired.

Next, suppose that there exists
a\in A
and
b\in B
for which
\|a-b\|_{2}\leq k
. We show that the reduction returns
\mathsf{true}
with probability at least
1-1/n
. Let
G
denote the
f
-graph on
C\cup D
. By definition of
C
and
D
and the fact that
f
is decreasing, there exists a pair of points in
C
and
D
with edge weight at least
f(x_{0})
.

By the lower bound on Proposition
8.10
,

\displaystyle\exists p\in C,q\in D\text{\penalty\ s.t.\penalty\ }\mathtt{Reff}_{G}(p,q)\leq 1/f(x_{0}).

By the upper bound of Proposition
8.9
,

\displaystyle b_{pq}^{\top}(L_{G}^{\dagger})^{2}b_{pq}\leq n\cdot(\mathtt{Reff}_{G}(p,q))^{2}\leq n/f(x_{0})^{2}.

Let
Z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell\times n}
be defined as
Z=P\cdot L_{G}^{\dagger}
. By Theorem
3.14
with
\varepsilon=1/2
applied to the collection of vectors
\{L_{G}^{\dagger}b_{s}\}_{s\in C\cup D}
and projection matrix
P
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|L_{G}^{\dagger}b_{st}\|_{2}\leq\|Zb_{st}\|_{2}\leq\frac{3}{2}\|L_{G}^{\dagger}b_{st}\|_{2}

for all pairs
s,t\in C\cup D
with high probability. Therefore, the second input guarantee of Proposition
8.11
is satisfied with high probability. Furthermore, for each
i\in[\ell]
,
\widetilde{z}_{i}
, the
i
th row of
\widetilde{Z}
, satisfies the first input guarantee by the output error guarantee of the algorithm
\mathcal{A}
. Therefore, Proposition
8.11
applies and shows that

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{Z}\cdot b_{pq}\|_{2}\leq\frac{9}{4}\|L_{G}^{\dagger}\cdot b_{pq}\|_{2}\leq 3\sqrt{n}/f(x_{0}).

This means that there exists of vectors
a\in\widehat{C},b\in\widehat{D}
with

\displaystyle\|a-b\|_{2}\leq 3\sqrt{n}/f(x_{0}).

By the approximation guarantee of the nearest neighbors data structure,
t\leq(3\sqrt{n}/f(x_{0}))\log n
and the reduction returns
\mathsf{true}
with probability at least
1-1/n
, as desired.

Next, suppose that there do not exist
a\in A
and
b\in B
for which
\|a-b\|_{2}\leq k
. We show that the reduction returns
\mathsf{false}
with probability at least
1-1/n
. Since
k\in S
and
A\cup B
is
\rho
-spaced,
\|a-b\|_{2}\geq\rho\cdot k
for all
a\in A
and
b\in B
. Therefore, all edges between
C
and
D
in the
f
-graph
G
for
C\cup D
have weight at most
f(\rho x_{0})\leq\frac{f(x_{0})}{100n^{16}}
since
f
is not
(\rho,L)
-multiplicatively Lipschitz.

By the upper bound of Proposition
8.10
,

\displaystyle\mathtt{Reff}_{G}(s,t)\geq\frac{1}{n^{2}f(\rho x_{0})}\geq\frac{100n^{14}}{f(x_{0})}

for any pair of vertices
s\in C,t\in D
.

By the lower bound of Proposition
8.9
,

\displaystyle b_{st}^{\top}(L_{G}^{\dagger})^{2}b_{st}\geq(\mathtt{Reff}_{G}(s,t))^{2}/2\geq(\frac{50n^{14}}{f(x_{0})})^{2}

for all
s\in C,t\in D
.

Recall from the discussion of the
\|a-b\|\leq k
case that Theorem
3.14
and Proposition
8.11
apply. Therefore, with probability at least
1-1/n
, by the lower bound of Proposition
8.11
,

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{Z}b_{st}\|_{2}\geq\frac{1}{4}\|L_{G}^{\dagger}b_{st}\|_{2}\geq\frac{12n^{14}}{f(x_{f})}

for any
s\in C,t\in D
.

Therefore, by the approximate nearest neighbors guarantee,

\displaystyle t\geq\frac{12n^{14}}{(\log n)\cdot f(x_{0})}>\frac{3\sqrt{n}(\log n)}{f(x_{0})},

so the algorithm returns
\mathsf{false}
with probability at least
1-1/n
, as desired.
∎

We now prove the theorems:

Proof of Theorem
8.6
.

Consider an instance of bichromatic
\ell_{2}
-closest pair for
n=L^{1/(32c_{0})}
and
d=c^{\log^{*}n}
, where
c
is the constant given in the dimension bound of Theorem
3.25
and
c_{0}
is such that integers have bit length
c_{0}\log n
in Theorem
3.25
. This consists of two sets of points
A,B\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
|A\cup B|=n
for which we wish to compute
\min_{a\in A,b\in B}\|a-b\|_{2}
. By Theorem
3.25
, the coordinates of points in
A
are also
c_{0}\log n
bit integers. Therefore, the set
S
of possible
\ell_{2}
distances between points in
A
and
B
is a set of square roots of integers with
\log d+c_{0}\log n\leq 2c_{0}\log n
bits. Therefore,
S
is a
\rho
-discrete (recall
\rho=1+(16\log(10n))/L
) since
1+1/n^{2c_{0}}>1+(16\log n)/L
. Furthermore, note that
f
is not
(\rho,L)
-multiplicatively Lipschitz by assumption.

We now describe an algorithm for solving
\ell_{2}
-closest pair on
A\times B
. Use binary search on the values in
S
to compute the minimum distance between points in
A,B
.
S
consists of integers between 1 and
n^{c_{0}}
(pairs with distance 0 can be found in linear time), so
\gamma\leq n^{c_{0}}
. For each query point
k\in S
, by Lemma
8.8
, there is a

\displaystyle O((\log n)\cdot\mathcal{T}(n,L,g(\gamma),d)+n)=O((\log n)\cdot\mathcal{T}(L^{1/(32c_{0})},L,L^{1/32},c^{\log^{*}L})+L^{1/(32c_{0})})

-time algorithm for determining whether or not the closest pair has distance at most
k
. Therefore, there is a

\displaystyle O((\log n)\mathcal{T}(L^{1/(32c_{0})},L,L^{1/32},c^{\log^{*}L})+L^{1/(32c_{0})})=\widetilde{O}(L^{1/(32c_{0})}L^{1/(64c_{0})}\log(L^{1/32}))<\widetilde{O}(n^{3/2})

time algorithm for solving
\ell_{2}
-closest pair on pairs of sets with
n
points. But this is impossible given
SETH
by Theorem
3.25
, a contradiction. This completes the result.
∎

Proof of Theorem
8.7
.

Consider an instance of bichromatic Hamming nearest neighbor search for
n=2^{L^{0.49}}
and
d=c_{1}\log n
for the constant
c_{1}
in the dimension bound in Theorem
3.21
. This consists of two sets of points
A,B\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
|A\cup B|=n
for which we wish to compute
\min_{a\in A,b\in B}\|a-b\|_{2}
. The coordinates of points in
A
and
B
are 0-1. Therefore, the set
S
of possible
\ell_{2}
distances between points in
A
and
B
is the set of square roots of integers between 0 and
c_{1}\log n
, which differ by a factor of at least
1+1/(16c_{1}\log n)>\rho
(recall
\rho=1+(16\log(10n))/L
). Therefore,
A\cup B
is
\rho
-spaced. Note that
f
is not
(\rho,L)
-multiplicatively Lipschitz by assumption. Furthermore,
A\cup B
is
\sqrt{c_{1}\log n}\leq L^{.25}
-boxed.

We now give an algorithm for solving
\ell_{2}
-closest pair on
A\times B
. Use binary search on
S
. For each query
k\in S
, Lemma
8.8
implies that one can check if there is a pair with distance at most
k
in

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(n,L,d)=

\displaystyle\penalty\ O(\mathcal{T}(2^{L^{.49}},L,e^{L^{.25}},2^{c_{1}L^{.49}})+2^{L^{.49}})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ O(2^{L^{.49}+L^{.48}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ n^{1+o(1)}

time on pairs of sets with
n
points. But this is impossible given
SETH
by Theorem
3.21
. This completes the result.
∎

9
Fast Multipole Method

The fast multipole method (FMM) was described as one of the top-10 most important algorithms of the 20th century
[
DS00
]
. It is a numerical technique that was developed to speed up calculations of long-range forces in the
n
-body problem in physics. In 1987, FMM was first introduced by Greengard and Rokhlin
[
GR87
]
, based on the multipole expansion of the vector Helmholtz equation. By treating the interactions between far-away basis functions using the FMM, the corresponding matrix elements do not need to be explicitly computed or stored. This is technique allows us to improve the naive
O(n^{2})
matrix-vector multiplication time to
o(n^{2})
.

Since Greengard and Rokhlin invented FMM, the topic has attracted researchers from many different fields, including physics, math, and computer science
[
GR87
,
Gre88
,
GR88
,
GR89
,
Gre90
,
GS91
,
EMRV92
,
Gre94
,
GR96
,
BG97
,
Dar00
,
YDGD03
,
YDD04
,
Mar12
]
.

We first give a quick overview of the high-level ideas of FMM in Section
9.1
. In Section
9.2
, we provide a complete description and proof of correctness for the fast Gaussian transform, where the kernel function is the Gaussian kernel. Although a number of researchers have used FMM in the past, most of the previous papers about FMM either focus on the low-dimensional or low-error cases. We therefore focus on the superconstant-error, high dimensional case, and carefully analyze the joint dependence on
\varepsilon
and
d
. We believe that our presentation of the original proof in Section
9.2
is thus of independent interest to the community. In Section
9.4
, we give the analogous results for other kernel functions used in this paper.

9.1
Overview

We begin with a description of high-level ideas of the Fast Multipole Method (FMM). Let
\mathsf{K}:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
denote a kernel function. The inputs to the FMM are
N
sources
s_{1},s_{2},\cdots,s_{N}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and
M
targets
t_{1},t_{2},\cdots,t_{M}
. For each
i\in[N]
, the source
s_{i}
has a strength
q_{i}
. Suppose all sources are in a ‘box’
{\cal B}
and all the targets are in a ‘box’
{\cal C}
. The goal is to evaluate

\displaystyle u_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathsf{K}(s_{i},t_{j})q_{i},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \forall j\in[M]

Intuitively, if
\mathsf{K}
has some nice property (e.g. smooth), we can hope to approximate
\mathsf{K}
in the following sense

\displaystyle\mathsf{K}(s,t)\approx\sum_{p=0}^{P-1}B_{p}(s)\cdot C_{p}(t),\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ s\in{\cal B},t\in{\cal C}

where
P
is a small positive integer, usually called the
interaction rank
in the literature.

Now, we can construct
u_{i}
in two steps:

\displaystyle v_{p}=\sum_{i\in{\cal B}}B_{p}(s_{i})q_{i},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \forall p=0,1,\cdots,P-1,

and

\displaystyle\widetilde{u}_{j}=\sum_{p=0}^{P-1}C_{p}(t_{j})v_{p},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \forall i\in[M].

Intuitively, as long as
{\cal B}
and
{\cal C}
are well-separated, then
\widetilde{u}_{j}
is very good estimation to
u_{j}
even for small
P
, i.e.,
|\widetilde{u}_{j}-u_{j}|<\varepsilon
.

Recall that, at the beginning of this section, we assumed that all the sources are in the the same box
{\cal B}
and
{\cal C}
. This is not true in general. To deal with this, we can discretize the continuous space into a batch of boxes
{\cal B}_{1},{\cal B}_{2},\cdots
and
{\cal C}_{1},{\cal C}_{2},\cdots
. For a box
{\cal B}_{l_{1}}
and a box
{\cal C}_{l_{2}}
, if they are very far apart, then the interaction between points within them is small, and we can ignore it. If the two boxes are close, then we deal wit them efficiently by truncating the high order expansion terms in
\mathsf{K}
(only keeping the first
\log^{O(d)}(1/\varepsilon)
). For each box, we will see that the number of nearby relevant boxes is at most
\log^{O(d)}(1/\varepsilon)
.

9.2
\mathsf{K}(x,y)=\exp(-\|x-y\|_{2}^{2})
, Fast Gaussian transform

Given
N
vectors
s_{1},\cdots s_{N}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
,
M
vectors
t_{1},\cdots,t_{M}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and a strength vector
q\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, Greengard and Strain
[
GS91
]
provided a fast algorithm for evaluating discrete Gauss transform

\displaystyle G(t_{i})=\sum_{j=1}^{N}q_{j}e^{-\|t_{i}-s_{j}\|^{2}/\delta}

for
i\in[M]
in
O(M+N)
time. In this section, we re-prove the algorithm described in
[
GS91
]
, and determine the exact dependences on
\varepsilon
and
d
in the running time.

By shifting the origin and rescaling
\delta
, we can assume that the sources
s_{j}
and targets
t_{i}
all lie in the unit box
{\cal B}_{0}=[0,1]^{d}
.

Let
t
and
s
lie in
d
-dimensional Euclidean space
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, and consider the Gaussian

\displaystyle e^{-\|t-s\|_{2}^{2}}=e^{-\sum_{i=1}^{d}(t_{i}-s_{i})^{2}}

We begin with some definitions.

Definition 9.1

(one-dimensional Hermite polynomial)
.

The Hermite polynomials
\widetilde{h}_{n}:{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
is defined as follows

\displaystyle\widetilde{h}_{n}(t)=(-1)^{n}e^{t^{2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{n}}{\mathrm{d}t}e^{-t^{2}}

Definition 9.2

(one-dimensional Hermite function)
.

The Hermite functions
h_{n}:{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
is defined as follows

\displaystyle h_{n}(t)=e^{-t^{2}}\widetilde{h}_{n}(t)

We use the following Fact to simplify
e^{-(t-s)^{2}/\delta}
.

Fact 9.3
.

For
s_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
\delta>0
, we have

\displaystyle e^{-(t-s)^{2}/\delta}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\cdot\left(\frac{s-s_{0}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right)^{n}\cdot h_{n}\left(\frac{t-s_{0}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right)

and

\displaystyle e^{-(t-s)^{2}/\delta}=e^{-(t-s_{0})^{2}/\delta}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\cdot\left(\frac{s-s_{0}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right)^{n}\cdot\widetilde{h}_{n}\left(\frac{t-s_{0}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right).

Proof.

\displaystyle e^{-(t-s)^{2}/\delta}=

\displaystyle\penalty\ e^{-(t-s_{0}-(s-s_{0}))^{2}/\delta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\left(\frac{s-s_{0}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right)^{n}h_{n}\left(\frac{t-s_{0}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ e^{-(t-s_{0})^{2}/\delta}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\left(\frac{s-s_{0}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right)^{n}\widetilde{h}_{n}\left(\frac{t-s_{0}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right).

∎

Using Cramer’s inequality, we have the following standard bound.

Lemma 9.4
.

For any constant
K\leq 1.09
, we have

\displaystyle|\widetilde{h}_{n}(t)|\leq K\cdot 2^{n/2}\cdot\sqrt{n!}\cdot e^{t^{2}/2}

and

\displaystyle|h_{n}(t)|\leq K\cdot 2^{n/2}\cdot\sqrt{n!}\cdot e^{-t^{2}/2}.

Next, we will extend the above definitions and observations to the high dimensional case. To simplify the discussion, we define multi-index notation. A multi-index
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\cdots,\alpha_{d})
is a
d
-tuple of nonnegative integers, playing the role of a multi-dimensional index. For any multi-index
\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and any
t\in{\mathbb{R}}^{t}
, we write

\displaystyle\alpha!=\penalty\ \prod_{i=1}^{d}(\alpha_{i}!),\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ t^{\alpha}=\penalty\ \prod_{i=1}^{d}t_{i}^{\alpha_{i}},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ D^{\alpha}=\penalty\ \partial_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\partial_{2}^{\alpha_{2}}\cdots\partial_{d}^{\alpha_{d}}.

where
\partial_{i}
is the differentiatial operator with respect to the
i
-th coordinate in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
. For integer
p
, we say
\alpha\geq p
if
\alpha_{i}\geq p,\forall i\in[d]
.

We can now define:

Definition 9.5

(multi-dimensional Hermite polynomial)
.

We define function
\widetilde{H}_{\alpha}:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
as follows:

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}_{\alpha}(t)=\prod_{i=1}^{d}\widetilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}(t_{i}).

Definition 9.6

(multi-dimensional Hermite function)
.

We define function
H_{\alpha}:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
as follows:

\displaystyle H_{\alpha}(t)=\prod_{i=1}^{d}h_{\alpha_{i}}(t_{i}).

It is easy to see that
H_{\alpha}(t)=e^{-\|t\|_{2}^{2}}\cdot\widetilde{H}_{\alpha}(t)

The Hermite expansion of a Gaussian in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
is

\displaystyle e^{-\|t-s\|_{2}^{2}}=\sum_{\alpha\geq 0}\frac{(t-s_{0})^{\alpha}}{\alpha!}h_{\alpha}(s-s_{0}).

(4)

Cramer’s inequality generalizes to

Lemma 9.7

(Cramer’s inequality)
.

Let
K<(1.09)^{d}
, then

\displaystyle|\widetilde{H}_{\alpha}(t)|\leq K\cdot e^{\|t\|_{2}^{2}/2}\cdot 2^{\|\alpha\|_{1}/2}\cdot\sqrt{\alpha!}

and

\displaystyle|H_{\alpha}(t)|\leq K\cdot e^{-\|t\|_{2}^{2}/2}\cdot 2^{\|\alpha\|_{1}/2}\cdot\sqrt{\alpha!}

The Taylor series of
H_{\alpha}
is

\displaystyle H_{\alpha}(t)=\sum_{\beta\geq 0}\frac{(t-t_{0})^{\beta}}{\beta!}(-1)^{\|\beta\|_{1}}H_{\alpha+\beta}(t_{0})

(5)

9.2.1
Estimation

We first give a definition

Definition 9.8
.

Let
{\cal B}
denote a box with center
s_{\cal B}
and side length
r\sqrt{2\delta}
with
r<1
.
If source
s_{j}
is in box
{\cal B}
, we say
j\in{\cal B}
. Then the Gaussian evaluation from the sources in box
{\cal B}
is,

\displaystyle G(t)=\sum_{j\in{\cal B}}q_{j}\cdot e^{-\|t-s_{j}\|_{2}^{2}/\delta}.

The Hermite expansion of
G(t)
is

\displaystyle G(t)=\sum_{\alpha\geq 0}A_{\alpha}\cdot h_{\alpha}\left(\frac{t-s_{\cal B}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right),

(6)

where the coefficients
A_{\alpha}
are defined by

\displaystyle A_{\alpha}=\frac{1}{\alpha!}\sum_{j\in{\cal B}}q_{j}\cdot\left(\frac{s_{j}-s_{\cal B}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right)^{\alpha}

(7)

The rest of this section will present a batch of Lemmas that bound the error of the function truncated at certain degree of Taylor and Hermite expansion.

Lemma 9.9
.

Let
p
denote an integer, let
\mathrm{Err}_{H}(p)
denote the error after truncating the series
G(t)
(as defined in Def.
9.8
) after
p^{d}
terms, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Err}_{H}(p)=\sum_{\alpha\geq p}A_{\alpha}\cdot H_{\alpha}\left(\frac{t-s_{\cal B}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right).

Then we have

\displaystyle|\mathrm{Err}_{H}(p)|\leq K\cdot\sum_{j\in{\cal B}}|q_{j}|\cdot\left(\frac{1}{p!}\right)^{d/2}\cdot\left(\frac{r^{p+1}}{1-r}\right)^{d},

where
K=(1.09)^{d}
.

Proof.

Using Eq. (
4
) to expand each Gaussian (see Definition
9.8
) in the

\displaystyle G(t)=\sum_{j\in{\cal B}}q_{j}\cdot e^{-\|t-s_{j}\|_{2}^{2}/\delta}

into a Hermite series about
s_{\cal B}

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha\geq 0}\left(\frac{1}{\alpha!}\sum_{j\in{\cal B}}q_{j}\cdot\left(\frac{s_{j}-s_{\cal B}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right)^{\alpha}\right)H_{\alpha}\left(\frac{t-s_{\cal B}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right)

and swap the summation over
\alpha
and
j
to obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in{\cal B}}q_{j}\sum_{\alpha\geq 0}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\cdot\left(\frac{s_{j}-s_{\cal B}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right)^{\alpha}\cdot H_{\alpha}\left(\frac{t-s_{\cal B}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right)

The truncation error bound follows from Cramer’s inequality (Lemma
9.7
) and the formula for the tail of a geometric series.

∎

The next Lemma shows how to convert a Hermite expansion about
s_{\cal B}
into a Taylor expansion about
t_{\cal C}
. The Taylor series converges rapidly in a box of side length
r\sqrt{2\delta}
about
t_{\cal C}
, where
r<1
.

Lemma 9.10
.

The Hermite expansion of
G(t)
is

\displaystyle G(t)=\sum_{\alpha\geq 0}A_{\alpha}\cdot H_{\alpha}\left(\frac{t-s_{\cal B}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right)

has the following Taylor expansion, at an arbitrary point
t_{0}
:

\displaystyle G(t)=\sum_{\beta\geq 0}B_{\beta}\left(\frac{t-t_{0}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right)^{\beta}.

(8)

where the coefficients
B_{\beta}
are defined as

\displaystyle B_{\beta}=\frac{(-1)^{|\beta|}}{\beta!}\sum_{\alpha\geq 0}A_{\alpha}\cdot H_{\alpha+\beta}\left(\frac{s_{\cal B}-t_{0}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right).

(9)

Let
\mathrm{Err}_{T}(p)
denote the error by truncating the Taylor series after
p^{d}
terms, in the box
{\cal C}
with center
t_{\cal C}
and side length
r\sqrt{2\delta}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Err}_{T}(p)=\sum_{\beta\geq p}B_{\beta}\left(\frac{t-t_{\cal C}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right)^{\beta}

Then

\displaystyle|\mathrm{Err}_{T}(p)|\leq K\cdot Q_{B}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{p!}\right)^{d/2}\left(\frac{r^{p+1}}{1-r}\right)^{d}.

Proof.

Each Hermite function in Eq. (
6
) can be expanded into a Taylor series by means of Eq. (
5
). The expansion in Eq. (
8
) is obtained by swapping the order of summation.

The truncation error bound can be proved as follows. Using Eq. (
7
) for
A_{\alpha}
, we can rewrite
B_{\beta}
:

\displaystyle B_{\beta}=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \frac{(-1)^{|\beta|}}{\beta!}\sum_{\alpha\geq 0}A_{\alpha}H_{\alpha+\beta}\left(\frac{s_{\cal B}-t_{\cal C}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \frac{(-1)^{|\beta|}}{\beta!}\sum_{\alpha\geq 0}\left(\frac{1}{\alpha!}\sum_{j\in{\cal B}}q_{j}\left(\frac{s_{j}-s_{\cal B}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right)^{\alpha}\right)H_{\alpha+\beta}\left(\frac{s_{\cal B}-t_{\cal C}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \frac{(-1)^{|\beta|}}{\beta!}\sum_{j\in{\cal B}}q_{j}\sum_{\alpha\geq 0}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\left(\frac{s_{j}-s_{\cal B}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right)^{\alpha}\cdot H_{\alpha+\beta}\left(\frac{s_{\cal B}-t_{\cal C}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right)

By Eq. (
5
), the inner sum is the Taylor expansion of
H_{\beta}((s_{j}-t_{\cal C})/\sqrt{\delta})
. Thus

\displaystyle B_{\beta}=\frac{(-1)^{\|\beta\|_{1}}}{\beta!}\sum_{j\in{\cal B}}q_{j}\cdot H_{\beta}\left(\frac{s_{j}-t_{\cal C}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right)

and Cramer’s inequality implies

\displaystyle|B_{\beta}|\leq\frac{1}{\beta!}K\cdot Q_{B}2^{\|\beta\|_{1}/2}\sqrt{\beta!}\leq KQ_{B}\frac{2^{\|\beta\|_{1}/2}}{\sqrt{\beta!}}

The truncation error follows from summation the tail of a geometric series.
∎

For the purpose of designing our algorithm, we’d like to make a variant of Lemma
9.10
in which the Hermite series is truncated before converting it to a Taylor series. This means that in addition to truncating the Taylor series itself, we are also truncating the finite sum formula in Eq. (
9
) for the coefficients.

Lemma 9.11
.

Let
G(t)
be defined as Def
9.8
. For an integer
p
, let
G_{p}(t)
denote the Hermite expansion of
G(t)
truncated at
p
,

\displaystyle G_{p}(t)=\sum_{\alpha\leq p}A_{\alpha}H_{\alpha}\left(\frac{t-s_{\cal B}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right).

The function
G_{p}(t)
has the following Taylor expansion about an arbitrary point
t_{0}
:

\displaystyle G_{p}(t)=\sum_{\beta\geq 0}C_{\beta}\cdot\left(\frac{t-t_{0}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right)^{\beta},

where the the coefficients
C_{\beta}
are defined as

\displaystyle C_{\beta}=\frac{(-1)^{\|\beta\|_{1}}}{\beta!}\sum_{\alpha\leq p}A_{\alpha}\cdot H_{\alpha+\beta}\left(\frac{s_{\cal B}-t_{\cal C}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right).

(10)

Let
\mathrm{Err}_{T}(p)
denote the error in truncating the Taylor series after
p^{d}
terms, in the box
{\cal C}
with center
t_{\cal C}
and side length
r\sqrt{2\delta}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Err}_{T}(p)=\sum_{\beta\geq p}C_{\beta}\left(\frac{t-t_{\cal C}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right)^{\beta}.

Then, we have

\displaystyle|\mathrm{Err}_{T}(p)|\leq K^{\prime}\cdot Q_{B}\left(\frac{1}{p!}\right)^{d/2}\left(\frac{r^{p+1}}{1-r}\right)^{d}

where
K^{\prime}\leq 2K
and
r\leq 1/2
.

Proof.

We can write
C_{\beta}
in the following way:

\displaystyle C_{\beta}=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \frac{(-1)^{\|\beta\|_{1}}}{\beta!}\sum_{j\in{\cal B}}q_{j}\sum_{\alpha\leq p}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\left(\frac{s_{j}-s_{\cal B}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right)^{\alpha}\cdot H_{\alpha+\beta}\left(\frac{s_{\cal B}-t_{\cal C}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \frac{(-1)^{\|\beta\|_{1}}}{\beta!}\sum_{j\in{\cal B}}q_{j}\left(\sum_{\alpha\geq 0}-\sum_{\alpha>p}\right)\frac{1}{\alpha!}\left(\frac{s_{j}-s_{\cal B}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right)^{\alpha}\cdot H_{\alpha+\beta}\left(\frac{s_{\cal B}-t_{\cal C}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ B_{\beta}-\frac{(-1)^{\|\beta\|_{1}}}{\beta!}\sum_{j\in{\cal B}}q_{j}\sum_{\alpha>p}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\left(\frac{s_{j}-s_{\cal B}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right)^{\alpha}\cdot H_{\alpha+\beta}\left(\frac{s_{\cal B}-t_{\cal C}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ B_{\beta}+(C_{\beta}-B_{\beta})

Next, we have

\displaystyle|\mathrm{Err}_{T}(p)|\leq\left|\sum_{\beta\geq p}B_{\beta}\left(\frac{t-t_{\cal C}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right)^{\beta}\right|+\left|\sum_{\beta\geq p}(C_{\beta}-B_{\beta})\cdot\left(\frac{t-t_{\cal C}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right)^{\beta}\right|

(11)

Using Lemma
9.10
, we can upper bound the first term in the Eq. (
11
) by,

\displaystyle K\cdot Q_{B}\left(\frac{1}{p!}\right)^{d/2}\cdot\left(\frac{r^{p+1}}{1-r}\right)^{d}

To bound the second term in Eq. (
11
), we can do the following

\displaystyle\penalty\ \left|\sum_{\beta\geq p}(C_{\beta}-B_{\beta})\cdot\left(\frac{t-t_{\cal C}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right)^{\beta}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ Q_{B}\cdot\sum_{\beta\geq p}\left|\Big(\frac{t-t_{\cal C}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\Big)^{\beta}\right|\cdot\frac{1}{\beta!}\sum_{\alpha>p}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\left|\Big(\frac{s_{j}-s_{\cal B}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\Big)^{\alpha}\right|\cdot\left|H_{\alpha+\beta}\left(\frac{s_{\cal B}-t_{\cal C}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ KQ_{B}\sum_{\alpha>p}\sum_{\beta>p}\frac{r^{\|\alpha\|_{1}}}{\sqrt{\alpha!}}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{(\alpha+\beta)!}{\alpha!\beta!}}\cdot\frac{r^{\|\beta\|_{1}}}{\sqrt{\beta!}}

Finally, the proof is complete since we know that

\displaystyle\frac{(\alpha+\beta)!}{\alpha!\beta!}\leq 2^{\|\alpha+\beta\|_{1}}.

∎

The proof of the following Lemma is almost identical. We omit the details here.

Lemma 9.12
.

Let
G_{s_{j}}(t)
be defined as

\displaystyle G_{s_{j}}(t)=q_{j}\cdot e^{-\|t-s_{j}\|_{2}^{2}/\delta}

has the following Taylor expansion at
t_{\cal C}

\displaystyle G_{s_{j}}(t)=\sum_{\beta\geq 0}{\cal B}_{\beta}\left(\frac{t-t_{\cal C}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right)^{\beta},

where the coefficients
B_{\beta}
is defined as

\displaystyle B_{\beta}=q_{j}\cdot\frac{(-1)^{\|\beta\|_{1}}}{\beta!}\cdot H_{\beta}\left(\frac{s_{j}-t_{\cal C}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right)

and the error in truncation after
p^{d}
terms is

\displaystyle|\mathrm{Err}_{T}(p)|=\left|\sum_{\beta\geq p}B_{\beta}\left(\frac{t-t_{\cal C}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right)^{\beta}\right|\leq K\cdot q_{j}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{p!}\right)^{d/2}\cdot\left(\frac{r^{p+1}}{1-r}\right)^{d}

for
r<1
.

9.2.2
Algorithm

The algorithm is based on subdividing
B_{0}
into smaller boxes with sides of length
r\sqrt{2\delta}
parallel to the axes, for a fixed
r\leq 1/2
. We can then assign each source
s_{j}
to the box
{\cal B}
in which it lies and each target
t
, to the box
{\cal C}
in which it lies.

For each target box
{\cal C}
, we need to evaluate the total field due to sources in all boxes. Since boxed
{\cal B}
have side lengths
r\sqrt{2\delta}
, only a fixed number of source boxes
{\cal B}
can contribute more than
Q\varepsilon
to the field in a given target box
{\cal C}
, where
Q=\|q\|_{1}
and
\varepsilon
is the precision parameter. If we cut off the sum over all
{\cal B}
after including the
(2k+1)^{d}
nearest boxes to
{\cal C}
, it incurs an error which can be upper bounded as follows

\displaystyle\sum_{j:\|t-s_{j}\|_{\infty}\geq kr\sqrt{2\delta}}|q_{j}|\cdot e^{-\|t-s_{j}\|_{2}^{2}/\delta}\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sum_{j:\|t-s_{j}\|_{\infty}\geq kr\sqrt{2\delta}}|q_{j}|\cdot e^{-\|t-s_{j}\|_{\infty}^{2}/\delta}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sum_{j:\|t-s_{j}\|_{\infty}\geq kr\sqrt{2\delta}}|q_{j}|\cdot e^{-(k\cdot r\sqrt{2\delta})^{2}/\delta}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ Q\cdot e^{-2r^{2}k^{2}}

(12)

where the first step follows from
\|\cdot\|_{2}\geq\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
, the second step follows from
\|t-s_{j}\|_{\infty}\geq kr\sqrt{2\delta}
, and the last step follows from a straightforward calculation.

For a box
{\cal B}
and a box
{\cal C}
, there are several possible ways to evaluate the interaction between
{\cal B}
and
{\cal C}
. We mainly need the following three techniques:

1.

N_{\cal B}
Gaussians, accumulated in Taylor series via definition
B_{\beta}
in Lemma
9.12

2.

Hermite series, directly evaluated

3.

Hermite series, accumulated in Taylor series in Lemma
9.11

Essentially, having any two of the above three techniques is sufficient to give an algorithm that runs in
(M+N)\log^{O(d)}(\|q\|_{1}/\varepsilon)
time.

In the next a few paragraphs, we explain the details of the three techniques.

Technique 1.

Consider a fixed source box
{\cal B}
. For each target box
{\cal C}
within range, we must compute
p^{d}
Taylor series coefficients

\displaystyle C_{\beta}({\cal B})=\frac{(-1)^{|\beta|}}{\beta!}\sum_{j\in{\cal B}}H_{\beta}\left(\frac{s_{j}-t_{C}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right).

Each coefficient requires
O(N_{\cal B})
work to evaluate, resulting in a net cost
O(p^{d}N_{\cal B})
. Since there are at most
(2k+1)^{d}
boxes within range, the total work for forming all the Taylor series is
O((2k+1)^{d}p^{d}N)
. Now, for each target
t_{i}
, one must evaluate the
p^{d}
-term Taylor series corresponding to the box in which
t_{i}
lies. The total running time of algorithm is thus

\displaystyle O((2k+1)^{d}p^{2}N)+O(p^{d}M).

Technique 2.

We form a Hermite series for each box
{\cal B}
and evaluate it at all targets. Using Lemma
9.9
, we can rewrite
G(t)
as

\displaystyle G(t)=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sum_{{\cal B}}\sum_{j\in{\cal B}}q_{j}\cdot e^{-\|t-s_{j}\|_{2}^{2}/\delta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sum_{{\cal B}}\sum_{\alpha\geq 0}A_{\alpha}(B)H_{\alpha}\left(\frac{t-s_{\cal B}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right)+\mathrm{Err}_{H}(p)

where
|\mathrm{Err}_{H}(p)|\leq\varepsilon
and

\displaystyle A_{\alpha}({\cal B})=\frac{1}{\alpha!}\sum_{j\in{\cal B}}q_{j}\cdot\left(\frac{s_{j}-s_{\cal B}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right)^{\alpha}.

(13)

To compute each
A_{\alpha}({\cal B})
costs
O(N_{\cal B})
time, so forming all the Hermite expansions takes
O(p^{d}N)
time. Evaluating at most
(2k+1)^{d}
expansions at each target
t_{i}
costs
O((2k+1)^{d}p^{d})
time per target, so this approach takes

\displaystyle O(p^{d}N)+O((2k+1)^{d}p^{d}M)

time in total.

Technique 3.

Let
N(B)
denote the number of boxes. Note that
N(B)\leq\min((r\sqrt{2\delta})^{-d/2},M)
.

Suppose we accumulate all sources into truncated Hermite expansions and transform all Hermite expansions into Taylor expansions via Lemma
9.11
. Then we can approximate the function
G(t)
by

\displaystyle G(t)=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sum_{{\cal B}}\sum_{j\in{\cal B}}q_{j}\cdot e^{-\|t-s_{j}\|_{2}^{2}/\delta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sum_{\beta\leq p}C_{\beta}\left(\frac{t-t_{\cal C}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right)^{\beta}+\mathrm{Err}_{T}(p)+\mathrm{Err}_{H}(p)

where
|\mathrm{Err}_{H}(p)|+|\mathrm{Err}_{T}(p)|\leq Q\cdot\varepsilon
,

\displaystyle C_{\beta}=\frac{(-1)^{\|\beta\|_{1}}}{\beta!}\sum_{{\cal B}}\sum_{\alpha\leq p}A_{\alpha}({\cal B})H_{\alpha+\beta}\left(\frac{s_{{\cal B}}-t_{{\cal C}}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right)

and the coefficients
A_{\alpha}({\cal B})
are defined as Eq. (
13
). Recall in Part 2, it takes
O(p^{d}N)
time to compute all the Hermite expansions, i.e., to compute the coefficients
A_{\alpha}({\cal B})
for all
\alpha\leq p
and all sources boxes
{\cal B}
.

Making use of the large product in the definition of
H_{\alpha+\beta}
, we see that the time to compute the
p^{d}
coefficients of
C_{\beta}
is only
O(dp^{d+1})
for each box
{\cal B}
in the range. Thus, we know for each target box
{\cal C}
, the running time is

\displaystyle O((2k+1)^{d}dp^{d+1}).

Finally we need to evaluate the appropriate Taylor series for each target
t_{i}
, which can be done in
O(p^{d}M)
time. Putting it all together, this technique 3 takes time

\displaystyle O((2k+1)^{d}dp^{d+1}N(B))+O(p^{d}N)+O(p^{d}M).

9.2.3
Result

Finally, in order to get
\varepsilon
additive error for each coordinate, we will choose
k=O(\log(\|q\|_{1}/\varepsilon))
and
p=O(\log(\|q\|_{1}/\varepsilon))
.

Theorem 9.13

(fast Gaussian transform)
.

Given
N
vectors
s_{1},\cdots,s_{N}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
,
M
vectors
t_{1},t_{2},\cdots,t_{M}

\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, a number
\delta>0
, and a vector
q\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, let function
G:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
be defined as
G(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}q_{i}\cdot e^{-\|t-s_{i}\|_{2}^{2}/\delta}
.
There is an algorithm that runs in

\displaystyle O\left((M+N)\log^{O(d)}(\|q\|_{1}/\varepsilon)\right)

time, and outputs
M
numbers
x_{1},\cdots,x_{M}
such that for each
j\in[M]

\displaystyle G(t_{j})-\varepsilon\leq x_{j}\leq G(t_{j})+\varepsilon.

The proof of fast Gaussian transform also implies a result for the online version:

Theorem 9.14

(online version)
.

Given
N
vectors
s_{1},\cdots,s_{N}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, a number
\delta>0
, and a vector
q\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, let function
G:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
be defined as
G(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}q_{i}\cdot e^{-\|t-s_{i}\|_{2}^{2}/\delta}
.
There is an algorithm that takes

\displaystyle O\left(N\log^{O(d)}(\|q\|_{1}/\varepsilon)\right)

time to do preprocessing, and then for each
t\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, takes
O(\log^{O(d)}(\|q\|_{1}/\varepsilon))
time to output a number
x
such that

\displaystyle G(t)-\varepsilon\leq x\leq G(t)+\varepsilon.

9.3
Generalization

The fast multipole method described in the previous section works not only for the Gaussian kernel, but also for
\mathsf{K}(u,v)=f(\|u,v\|_{2}^{2})
for many other functions
f
. As long as
f
has the following properties, the result of Theorem
9.13
also holds for
f
:

•

f:{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
.

•

f
is non-increasing, i.e., if
x\geq y\geq 0
, then
f(x)\leq f(y)
.

•

f
is decreasing fast, i.e., for any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, we have
f(\Theta(\log(1/\varepsilon)))\leq\varepsilon
.

•

f
’s Hermite expansion and Taylor expansions are truncateable: If we only keep
\log^{d}(1/\varepsilon)
terms of the polynomial for
\mathsf{K}
, then the error is at most
\varepsilon
.

Let us now sketch how each of these properties is used in discritizing the continuous domain into a finite number of boxes. First, note that Eq.(
9.2.2
) holds more generally for any function
f
with these properties. Indeed, we can bound the error as follows (note that
Q=\|q\|_{1}
):

\displaystyle\sum_{j:\|t-s_{j}\|_{\infty}\geq kr\sqrt{2\delta}}|q_{j}|\cdot f(\|t-s_{j}\|_{2}/\sqrt{\delta})\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sum_{j:\|t-s_{j}\|_{\infty}\geq kr\sqrt{2\delta}}|q_{j}|\cdot f(\|t-s_{j}\|_{\infty}/\sqrt{\delta})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sum_{j:\|t-s_{j}\|_{\infty}\geq kr\sqrt{2\delta}}|q_{j}|\cdot f(\sqrt{2}kr)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ Q\cdot f(\sqrt{2}kr)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ \varepsilon

(14)

where the first step follows from
\|\cdot\|_{2}\geq\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
and that
f
is non-increasing, the second step follows from
\|t-s_{j}\|_{\infty}\geq kr\sqrt{2\delta}
and that
f
is non-increasing, and the last step follows from the fact that
f
is decreasing fast, and choosing
k=O(\log(Q/\varepsilon)/r)
.

We next give an example of how the truncatable expansions property is used. Here we only show to generalize Definition
9.8
to Definition
9.15
and generalize Lemma
9.9
to Definition
9.16
; the other Lemmas in the proof can be extended in a similar way.

Definition 9.15
.

Let
{\cal B}
denote a box with center
s_{\cal B}
and side length
r\sqrt{2\delta}
with
r<1
.
If source
s_{j}
is in box
{\cal B}
, we say
j\in{\cal B}
. Then the Gaussian evaluation from the sources in box
{\cal B}
is,

\displaystyle G(t)=\sum_{j\in{\cal B}}q_{j}\cdot f(\|t-s_{j}\|_{2}/\sqrt{\delta}).

The Hermite expansion of
G(t)
is

\displaystyle G(t)=\sum_{\alpha\geq 0}A_{\alpha}\cdot h_{\alpha}\left(\frac{t-s_{\cal B}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right),

(15)

where the coefficients
A_{\alpha}
are defined by

\displaystyle A_{\alpha}=\frac{1}{\alpha!}\sum_{j\in{\cal B}}q_{j}\cdot\left(\frac{s_{j}-s_{\cal B}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right)^{\alpha}

(16)

Definition 9.16
.

Let
p
denote an integer, let
\mathrm{Err}_{H}(p)
denote the error after truncating the series
G(t)
(as defined in Def.
9.15
) after
p^{d}
terms, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Err}_{H}(p)=\sum_{\alpha\geq p}A_{\alpha}\cdot H_{\alpha}\left(\frac{t-s_{\cal B}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\right).

We say
f
is Hermite truncateable, if
Then we have

\displaystyle|\mathrm{Err}_{H}(p)|\leq p^{-\Omega(pd)}

where
K=(1.09)^{d}
.

9.4
\mathsf{K}(x,y)=1/\|x-y\|_{2}^{2}

Similar ideas yield the following algorithm:

Theorem 9.17

(FMM,
[
BG97
,
Mar12
]
)
.

Given
n
vectors
x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{n}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, let matrix
A\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times n}
be defined as
A_{i,j}=1/\|x_{i}-x_{j}\|_{2}^{2}
. For any vector
h\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, in time
O(n\log^{O(d)}(\|u\|_{1}/\varepsilon))
, we can output a vector
u
such that

\displaystyle(Ah)_{i}-\varepsilon\leq u_{i}\leq(Ah)_{i}+\varepsilon.

10
Neural Tangent Kernel

In this section, we show that the popular Neural Tangent Kernel
\mathsf{K}
from theoretical Deep Learning can be rearranged into the form
\mathsf{K}(x,y)=f(\|x-y\|_{2}^{2})
for an appropriate analytic function
f
, so our results in this paper apply to it.
We first define the kernel.

Definition 10.1

(Neural Tangent Kernel,
[
JGH18
]
)
.

Given
n
points
x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{n}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, and any activation function
\sigma:{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
, the neural tangent kernel matrix
\mathsf{K}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times n}
can be defined as follows, where
\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
denotes the Gaussian distribution:

\displaystyle\mathsf{K}_{i,j}:=\int_{\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})}\sigma^{\prime}(w^{\top}x_{i})\sigma^{\prime}(w^{\top}x_{j})x_{i}^{\top}x_{j}\mathrm{d}w.

In the literature of convergence results for deep neural networks
[
LL18
,
DZPS19
,
AZLS19b
,
AZLS19a
,
SY19
,
BPSW21
,
LSS+20
]
, it is natural to assume that all the data points are on the unit sphere, i.e., for all
i\in[n]
we have
\|x_{i}\|_{2}=1
and datas are separable i.e., for all
i\neq j
,
\|x_{i}-x_{j}\|_{2}\geq\delta
. One of the most standard and common used activation functions in neural network training is ReLU activation, which is
\sigma(x)=\max\{x,0\}
. Using Lemma
10.2
, we can figure out the corresponding kernel function. By Theorem
5.14
, the multiplication task for neural tangent kernels is hard. In neural network training, the multiplication can potentially being used to speed the neural network training procedure(See
[
SY19
]
).

In the following lemma, we compute the kernel function for ReLU activation function.

Lemma 10.2
.

If
\sigma(x)=\max\{x,0\}
, then the Neural Tangent Kernel can be written as
\mathsf{K}(x,y)=f(\|x-y\|_{2}^{2})
for

\displaystyle f(x)=\frac{1}{\pi}(\pi-\cos^{-1}(1-0.5x))\cdot(1-0.5x)

Proof.

First, since
\|x_{i}\|_{2}=\|x_{j}\|_{2}
, we know that

\displaystyle\|x_{i}-x_{j}\|_{2}^{2}=\|x_{i}\|_{2}^{2}-2\langle x_{i},x_{j}\rangle+\|x_{j}\|_{2}^{2}=2-2\langle x_{i},x_{j}\rangle.

By definition of
\sigma
, we know

\displaystyle\sigma(x)=\begin{cases}1,&\text{\penalty\ if\penalty\ }x>0;\\
0,&\text{\penalty\ otherwise.}\end{cases}

Using properties of the Gaussian distribution
{\cal N}(0,I_{d})
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})}\sigma^{\prime}(w^{\top}x_{i})\sigma^{\prime}(w^{\top}x_{j})\mathrm{d}w=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \frac{1}{\pi}(\pi-\cos^{-1}(x_{i}x_{j}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \frac{1}{\pi}(\pi-\cos^{-1}(1-0.5\|x_{i}-x_{j}\|_{2}^{2}))

We can rewrite
\mathsf{K}_{i,j}
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathsf{K}_{i,j}=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \int_{\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})}\sigma^{\prime}(w^{\top}x_{i})\sigma^{\prime}(w^{\top}x_{j})x_{i}^{\top}x_{j}\mathrm{d}w

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ (1-0.5\|x_{i}-x_{j}\|_{2}^{2})\cdot\int_{\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})}\sigma^{\prime}(w^{\top}x_{i})\sigma^{\prime}(w^{\top}x_{j})\mathrm{d}w

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ (1-0.5\|x_{i}-x_{j}\|_{2}^{2})\cdot\frac{1}{\pi}(\pi-\cos^{-1}(1-0.5x))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ f(\|x_{i}-x_{j}\|_{2}^{2}).

∎

Lemma 10.3
.

Given
n
data points
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
on unit sphere. For any activation function
\sigma:{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
, the corresponding Neural Tangent Kernel
\mathsf{K}(x_{i},x_{j})
is a function of
\|x_{i}-x_{j}\|_{2}^{2}
.

Proof.

Note that
w\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
, so we know
(x_{i}^{\top}w,x_{j}^{\top}w)\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{i,j})
, where the covariance matrix

\displaystyle\Sigma_{i,j}=\begin{bmatrix}x_{i}^{\top}x_{i}&x_{i}^{\top}x_{j}\\
x_{j}^{\top}x_{i}&x_{j}^{\top}x_{j}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}1&x_{i}^{\top}x_{j}\\
x_{i}^{\top}x_{j}&1\end{bmatrix}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2\times 2},

since
\|x_{i}\|_{2}=\|x_{j}\|_{2}=1
.
Thus,

\displaystyle\mathsf{K}(x_{i},x_{j})=\mathbf{E}_{(a,b)\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{i,j})}[\sigma^{\prime}(a)\sigma^{\prime}(b)]x_{i}^{\top}x_{j}=g(x_{i}^{\top}x_{j})

for some function
g
.

Note
x_{i}^{\top}x_{j}=-\frac{1}{2}\|x_{i}-x_{j}\|^{2}+1
, so
\mathsf{K}(x_{i},x_{j})=f(\|x_{i}-x_{j}\|_{2}^{2})
for some function
f
, which completes the proof.
∎
